def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right;  simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left.   The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other.  All cars move in the same speed.  Two cars are said to collide
    when a car that's moving left to right hits a car that's moving right to left.
    However, the cars are infinitely sturdy and strong; as a result, they continue moving
    in their trajectory as if they did not collide.

    This function outputs the number of such collisions.
    """

mutated prompt:

    0
    >>> fibfib(5)
    4
    >>> fibfib(8)
    24
    """

def pluck(arr):
    """
    "Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has non-negative integer nodes
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smalest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []
    
    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.

    Constraints:
        * 1 <= nodes.length <= 10000
        * 0 <= node.value
    """

mutated prompt:
def pluck(arr):
    """
    "You are given an array that represents a branch of a tree with nodes that are non-negative integers.
    The task is to pick one of these nodes and return it.
    The chosen node should have the smallest even value.
    If there are multiple nodes with this smallest even value, return the one with the lowest index.

    The result should be returned in the form of a list, [smallest_value, its index].
    If there are no even values or the array provided is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 is the smallest even value, and it has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 is the smallest even value, and it has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []
    
    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but there are two zeros,
                     so the first occurrence is chosen as it has the smallest index.

    Constraints:
        * 1 <= nodes.length <= 10000
        * 0 <= node.value
    """

        search([5, 5, 4, 4, 4]) == -1
    '''

      arr = []

            -> reverse arr -> [55, 1, -1]
      return = ['One']
    """

def move_one_ball(arr):
    """We have an array 'arr' of N integers arr[1], arr[2], ..., arr[N].The
    numbers in the array will be randomly ordered. Your task is to determine if
    it is possible to get an array sorted in non-decreasing order by performing 
    the following operation on the given array:
        You are allowed to perform right shift operation any number of times.
    
    One right shift operation means shifting all elements of the array by one
    position in the right direction. The last element of the array will be moved to
    the starting position in the array i.e. 0th index. 

    If it is possible to obtain the sorted array by performing the above operation
    then return True else return False.
    If the given array is empty then return True.

    Note: The given list is guaranteed to have unique elements.

    For Example:
    
    move_one_ball([3, 4, 5, 1, 2])==>True
    Explanation: By performin 2 right shift operations, non-decreasing order can
                 be achieved for the given array.
    move_one_ball([3, 5, 4, 1, 2])==>False
    Explanation:It is not possible to get non-decreasing order for the given
                array by performing any number of right shift operations.
                
    """

mutated prompt:
def move_one_ball(arr):
    """We have an array 'arr' consisting of N integers arr[1], arr[2], ..., arr[N].
    The elements in the array are arranged randomly. Your goal is to find out if
    it is feasible to sort the array in non-decreasing order by executing 
    the described operation on the provided array:
        You are permitted to carry out right shift operations as many times as needed.
    
    A right shift involves moving all elements of the array one position to the right.
    The element at the last position will shift to the beginning of the array at the 0th index.

    If the sorted array can be achieved by using the above operation,
    then return True; otherwise, return False.
    If the input array is empty, then return True.

    Note: The array is guaranteed to have unique elements.

    For Example:
    
    move_one_ball([3, 4, 5, 1, 2])==>True
    Explanation: By executing 2 right shift operations, the array can
                 be transformed into non-decreasing order.
    move_one_ball([3, 5, 4, 1, 2])==>False
    Explanation: It is impossible to rearrange the array into non-decreasing order
                using any amount of right shift operations.
                
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Take a positive integer n and produce a sorted list containing the odd numbers from its Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a hypothesis in mathematics which involves a sequence that is generated as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each subsequent term is produced from the previous one in the sequence. If the previous term is an even number, the next term is half of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is three times the previous term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list is ordered in ascending fashion.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], hence the only odd numbers are 1 and 5.
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture defines a sequence: start with any positive integer n. If the term is even, the next term is half of it. If the term is odd, the next term is 3 times the term plus 1. The sequence eventually reaches 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. The returned list is sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are 1, and 5.
    """
```

 ```python
def do_algebra(operator, operand):
    """
    You have two lists to work with: operator and operand. The first list holds basic mathematics operations, 
    while the second list contains a sequence of integers. You are to use these lists to form an algebraic 
    expression and produce the evaluated result.

    The basic mathematics operations:
    Addition ( + ) 
    Subtraction ( - ) 
    Multiplication ( * ) 
    Floor division ( // ) 
    Exponentiation ( ** ) 

    Example:
    operator['+', '*', '-']
```

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list containing the odd numbers from the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is an idea in mathematics about a sequence defined as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each term is computed from the 
    previous term using these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that regardless of the initial value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) gives [1].
        2. The returned list is sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], and the odd numbers are 1 and 5.
    """
```

def valid_date(date):
    """You have to write a function which validates a given date string and
    returns True if the date is valid otherwise False.
    The date is valid if all of the following rules are satisfied:
    1. The date string is not empty.
    2. The number of days is not less than 1 or higher than 31 days for months 1,3,5,7,8,10,12. And the number of days is not less than 1 or higher than 30 days for months 4,6,9,11. And, the number of days is not less than 1 or higher than 29 for the month 2.
    3. The months should not be less than 1 or higher than 12.
    4. The date should be in the format: mm-dd-yyyy

    for example: 
    valid_date('03-11-2000') => True

    valid_date('15-01-2012') => False

    triples_sum_to_zero takes a list of integers as its argument.

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, generate a sorted list containing the odd numbers from the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a hypothesis in mathematics about a specific sequence characterized
    by the following rules: starting with any positive integer n, each subsequent term is derived 
    from the previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is three times the previous
    term plus 1. The hypothesis suggests that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. The returned list is sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture involves a sequence defined as: start with any positive integer n. 
    If a term is even, the next term is half of it. If a term is odd, the next term is 3 times 
    the term plus 1. The conjecture is that the sequence will always reach 1, regardless of n.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. Returned list is sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], resulting in odd numbers 1 and 5.
    """

def hex_key(num):
    """Create a function that takes a hexadecimal number represented as a string 
    and determines how many of its digits are prime numbers (a prime number is defined 
    as a natural number greater than 1 that cannot be formed by multiplying two smaller

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a list in ascending order containing the odd numbers from the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a theory in mathematics regarding a sequence defined in this way: begin with any positive integer n. Each subsequent term is generated from the preceding term as follows: if the preceding term is even, the next term is half of the preceding term. If the preceding term is odd, the next term is three times the preceding term plus one. The conjecture suggests that irrespective of the initial n, the sequence eventually reaches 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list containing the odd numbers from the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a mathematical proposal that deals with a sequence defined
    as follows: begin with any positive integer n. For each subsequent term, derive it from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is half the 
    previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture asserts that regardless of the initial value of n, the sequence will ultimately reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.

    Implement the function that fulfills the requirements described.
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture involves a sequence defined as follows: start with any positive integer n. If the previous term is even, the next term is half of the previous term. If odd, the next term is 3 times the previous term plus 1. No matter the starting value, the sequence will reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. Return a sorted list in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], with odd numbers 1 and 5.
    """

 def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.

    Implement the function that fulfills the requirements described.

    Ensure that the function handles inputs up to at least 10,000 efficiently.
    """
mutated prompt:
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in its Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture involves a sequence: start with n. If a term is even, the next is half of it. If odd, the next is 3 times it plus 1. The conjecture states the sequence always reaches 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. Returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.

    Implement the function efficiently for inputs up to at least 10,000.
    """

 def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in its Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture involves a sequence: start with n. If a term is even, the next is half of it. If odd, the next is 3 times it plus 1. The conjecture states the sequence always reaches 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. Returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.

    Implement the function efficiently for inputs up to at least 10,000.
    """

    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smalest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []
    
    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.

    Constraints:
        * 1 <= nodes.length <= 10000
        * 0 <= node.value
    """
